It is invasive in parts of the United States and Canada, where it can form dense growth that displaces native plants and degrades wildlife habitat. Early detection and complete removal of its rhizomes and seed pods are recommended; mowing limits seed production but does not eliminate established plants. [s1:p8–s1:p13, s1:p15–s1:p16]

Stachys circinata is a perennial-looking herbaceous species in the mint family (Lamiaceae), described by L’Héritier in 1786. Sequoia sempervirens · 47372
FAIL: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)
No summary returned.
Human edibility: not returned — 
Original output
The coast redwood (*Sequoia sempervirens*) is the sole living species in its genus and a long-lived evergreen conifer native to a narrow coastal belt from central California to southwestern Oregon. It can live for roughly 2,000 years and is the world’s tallest living tree species, reaching about 116 meters (381 feet). Coast redwoods thrive in cool, moist forests, absorb water from coastal fog, and can regenerate from seeds, stumps, roots, and fallen branches. Their thick bark provides strong resistance to fire, insects, fungi, and decay. The lightweight, durable wood is commercially valuable, though extensive logging has greatly reduced the species’ original old-growth forests. (Sources: s1:p2–p5, p13–p14, p27, p30, p33, p39–p43)

Anredera cordifolia · 75457
FAIL: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)
No summary returned.
Human edibility: not returned — 
Original output
Anredera cordifolia (Madeira or mignonette vine) is a fast-growing, evergreen succulent climber native to South America. Its heart-shaped fleshy leaves, fragrant cream flowers, rhizomes, and easily detached aerial tubers help identify it. Introduced widely, it is highly invasive and can smother vegetation and collapse mature trees.

The leaves and stems may be eaten raw or cooked, and the rhizomes are edible; the aerial bulbils are inedible. Leaves contain beta-carotene, vitamin E, calcium, and mucilage. Because fragments readily form new plants, discarded material should never be composted or placed in green waste. (Sources: s1:p1, p3–p8, p14–p16, p18, p24–p25)

Creeping gloxinia (*Lophospermum erubescens*) is a climbing or sprawling perennial native to Mexico’s Sierra Madre Oriental. It uses twining leaf stalks to climb and bears tubular pinkish-red flowers, typically from April through January in its native habitat. Widely grown as an ornamental since 1830, it prefers frost-free conditions but can survive colder winters if its roots and base are protected. It has naturalized across many tropical and subtropical regions. Despite its common name, it is not closely related to true gloxinias. [Sources: s1:p1, s1:p3–p5, s1:p11–p14]

Alternanthera philoxeroides · 75386
FAIL: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)

Human edibility: not returned — 
Original output
Alligatorweed (*Alternanthera philoxeroides*) is a South American perennial herb now invasive in more than 30 countries. It grows on land and in shallow water, where its hollow, sprawling stems form dense floating mats; it has opposite leaves and small, white flowers on stalked spikes (s1:p1–p2).

These mats displace native vegetation, obstruct waterways and irrigation, degrade habitat, and increase flooding and mosquito-breeding risks (s1:p3–p6). Outside its native range, alligatorweed spreads chiefly through stem and leaf fragments, so incomplete manual removal can worsen dispersal (s1:p7–p9). Management includes early detection, physical containment, biological-control insects, and repeated herbicide applications (s1:p11–p12). Import and trade are prohibited throughout the European Union (s1:p3).

Ulmus parvifolia · 79462
FAIL: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)

Human edibility: not returned — 
Original output
The Chinese elm (*Ulmus parvifolia*), also called lacebark elm, is a small-to-medium deciduous or semideciduous tree native to eastern Asia. It typically grows 10–18 m tall and has small, glossy leaves, autumn-produced flowers and samaras, and distinctive flaking, mottled bark [s1:p1–p2].

Widely cultivated as a hardy ornamental and bonsai, it tolerates pruning and harsh urban conditions and shows strong resistance to Dutch elm disease and several pests [s1:p7, s1:p10–p12]. Its hard, tough wood is used for furniture, flooring, tool handles, veneer, and longbows [s1:p4–p5]. The species has naturalized in several countries and is considered invasive in parts of the United States [s1:p23].

Podocarpus totara · 85275
FAIL: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)

Original output
Tōtara (*Podocarpus totara*) is a long-lived podocarp tree endemic to New Zealand. It usually grows 20–25 m tall, occasionally exceeding 35 m, and has flaky purplish-to-golden-brown bark, stiff needle-like leaves, and fleshy red mature cones bearing one or two seeds (s1:p2, s1:p5).

It occurs across the North and South Islands, mainly in fertile, well-drained lowlands but also in montane and lower-subalpine habitats up to 600 m (s1:p15). Its durable, rot-resistant wood was especially important for Māori carving and waka construction and was later widely used for piles, railway sleepers, and fence posts (s1:p21–p22). The red flesh surrounding its seeds is edible (s1:p24), and tōtara symbolizes strength and mana in Māori culture (s1:p26).

Rustwort (*Nowellia curvifolia*) is a tiny, delicate liverwort found on rotting, bark-free logs across northern temperate and subarctic forests. **Turkish hazel (*Corylus colurna*)** is a large deciduous tree in the birch family, native to southeastern Europe and southwestern Asia. It can reach about 25–33 m tall and develops a broad, irregular crown with age.

Its edible hazelnuts ripen from September to October and taste similar to common hazelnuts, but their small size and thick shells limit commercial value. The species is widely planted as an ornamental and street tree because it tolerates urban conditions, drought, cold, heat, and alkaline soils. Its non-suckering growth also makes it a valuable rootstock for commercial hazelnut cultivars.

The Gymea lily (*Doryanthes excelsa*) is a striking flowering plant endemic to coastal New South Wales, Australia. It forms a rosette of long, sword-shaped leaves and produces a flower spike up to 6 m tall, crowned with large, bright-red flowers in spring. Itakah.

Original output
Cuphea carthagenensis (Colombian waxweed, also called sete-sangrias) is a South American herb in the family Lythraceae. It grows 20–60 cm tall, with reddish glandular hairs, opposite leaves, small pink-purple flowers, and capsule fruits. It prefers moist, sandy soils and can become invasive in fields, vacant lots, and pastures.

The whole plant is used in South American folk medicine, but its therapeutic effects remain poorly studied. Excessive consumption may cause diarrhea, prolonged use may sharply lower blood pressure, and use in children is not recommended.
